 1977: 
 1978: =head2 sending_software()
 1979: 
 1980: Returns information extracted from headers that identify the software or
 1981: server-side infrastructure used to compose or inject the message.  These
 1982: headers are injected by email clients, bulk-mailing libraries, and shared
 1983: hosting control panels, and are often the most direct evidence of how the
 1984: spam was sent and from which server.
 1985: 
 1986: Headers examined: C<X-Mailer>, C<User-Agent>, C<X-PHP-Originating-Script>,
 1987: C<X-Source>, C<X-Source-Args>, C<X-Source-Host>.
 1988: 
 1989: The C<X-PHP-Originating-Script>, C<X-Source>, and C<X-Source-Host> headers
 1990: in particular are injected automatically by many shared hosting providers
 1991: (cPanel, Plesk, DirectAdmin) and reveal the exact PHP script path and
 1992: hostname responsible.  A hosting abuse team can use these values to
 1993: identify the compromised or malicious account immediately, without needing
 1994: to search logs.
 1995: 
 1996: The data is extracted synchronously during C<parse_email()> with no network
 1997: I/O.  This method simply returns the pre-built list.
 1998: 
 1999: =head3 Usage
 2000: 
 2001:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 2002:     my @sw = $analyser->sending_software();
 2003: 
 2004:     for my $s (@sw) {
 2005:         printf "%-30s : %s\n", $s->{header}, $s->{value};
 2006:         printf "  Note: %s\n", $s->{note};
 2007:     }
 2008: 
 2009:     # Check for shared-hosting injection headers
 2010:     my @hosting = grep {
 2011:         $_->{header} =~ /^x-(?:php-originating-script|source)/
 2012:     } @sw;
 2013: 
 2014:     if (@hosting) {
 2015:         print "Shared-hosting script detected -- report to hosting abuse team:\n";
 2016:         print "  $_->{header}: $_->{value}\n" for @hosting;
 2017:     }
 2018: 
 2019:     # Extract the mailer name if present
 2020:     my ($mailer) = grep { $_->{header} eq 'x-mailer' } @sw;
 2021:     printf "Sent with: %s\n", $mailer->{value} if $mailer;
 2022: 
 2023: =head3 Arguments
 2024: 
 2025: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 2026: 
 2027: =head3 Returns
 2028: 
 2029: A list (not an arrayref) of hashrefs, one per recognised software-fingerprint
 2030: header that was present in the message, in alphabetical order of header name.
 2031: Returns an empty list if none of the watched headers are present, or if
 2032: C<parse_email()> has not been called.
 2033: 
 2034:     {
 2035:         header => 'X-PHP-Originating-Script',
 2036:         value  => '1000:newsletter.php',
 2037:         note   => 'PHP script on shared hosting - report to hosting abuse team',
 2038:     }
 2039: 
 2040: Each hashref contains exactly three keys, all always present:
 2041: 
 2042: =over 4
 2043: 
 2044: =item C<header> (string)
 2045: 
 2046: The header name, lower-cased.  One of the six values listed in the
 2047: Algorithm section below.
 2048: 
 2049: =item C<value> (string)
 2050: 
 2051: The header value exactly as it appeared in the message (not decoded or
 2052: transformed in any way).
 2053: 
 2054: =item C<note> (string)
 2055: 
 2056: A fixed, human-readable annotation describing what this header represents
 2057: and the recommended action.  The note string is determined by the header
 2058: name and is the same for all messages; it is not derived from the value.
 2059: See the Algorithm section for the note associated with each header.
 2060: 
 2061: =back
 2062: 
 2063: =head3 Side Effects
 2064: 
 2065: None.  All data is collected during C<parse_email()> and this method
 2066: only returns the pre-collected list.  No network I/O is performed.
 2067: 
 2068: =head3 Algorithm: headers examined
 2069: 
 2070: The following six headers are examined during C<parse_email()>.  They are
 2071: checked in alphabetical order; the result list preserves that order
 2072: (i.e. C<user-agent> appears before C<x-mailer> which appears before
 2073: C<x-php-originating-script>, etc.).  At most one entry per header name is
 2074: produced even if the header appears more than once; the first occurrence is
 2075: used.
 2076: 
 2077: =over 4
 2078: 
 2079: =item C<user-agent>
 2080: 
 2081: Note: C<"Email client identifier">
 2082: 
 2083: Set by some email clients (Thunderbird, Evolution) as an alternative to
 2084: C<X-Mailer>.  Identifies the application that composed the message.
 2085: 
 2086: =item C<x-mailer>
 2087: 
 2088: Note: C<"Email client or bulk-mailer identifier">
 2089: 
 2090: The most widely used header for identifying the sending application.
 2091: Values range from standard clients (C<"Apple Mail">, C<"Microsoft Outlook">)
 2092: to bulk-mailing libraries (C<"PHPMailer 6.0">, C<"MailMate">).  Its presence
 2093: in spam often reveals the library used to generate the campaign.
 2094: 
 2095: =item C<x-php-originating-script>
 2096: 
 2097: Note: C<"PHP script on shared hosting -- report to hosting abuse team">
 2098: 
 2099: Injected by cPanel and similar shared-hosting control panels when a PHP
 2100: script sends mail via the local MTA.  The value typically takes the form
 2101: C<uid:script.php> (e.g. C<"1000:newsletter.php">), directly identifying
 2102: the Unix user account and the script responsible.  This is the single most
 2103: actionable header for shared-hosting abuse reports.
 2104: 
 2105: =item C<x-source>
 2106: 
 2107: Note: C<"Source file on shared hosting -- report to hosting abuse team">
 2108: 
 2109: Also injected by shared-hosting platforms, typically containing the full
 2110: filesystem path to the sending script (e.g.
 2111: C<"/home/user/public_html/contact.php">).  Complements
 2112: C<X-PHP-Originating-Script>.
 2113: 
 2114: =item C<x-source-args>
 2115: 
 2116: Note: C<"Command-line args injected by shared hosting provider">
 2117: 
 2118: The command-line arguments of the process that sent the mail, injected by
 2119: some hosting platforms.  May reveal interpreter invocations or script
 2120: parameters useful for forensic analysis.
 2121: 
 2122: =item C<x-source-host>
 2123: 
 2124: Note: C<"Sending hostname injected by shared hosting provider">
 2125: 
 2126: The hostname of the server that submitted the message, injected by the
 2127: hosting platform.  Useful when the IP in the C<Received:> chain is a shared
 2128: outbound relay rather than the originating server.
 2129: 
 2130: =back
 2131: 
 2132: =head3 Notes
 2133: 
 2134: =over 4
 2135: 
 2136: =item *
 2137: 
 2138: The result list is reset to empty by each call to C<parse_email()>.  If no
 2139: watched headers are present in the current message, the list is empty.
 2140: 
 2141: =item *
 2142: 
 2143: The alphabetical ordering of entries is a side effect of iterating over
 2144: the C<%sw_notes> hash in sorted key order.  It is stable across calls on
 2145: the same message.
 2146: 
 2147: =item *
 2148: 
 2149: Header names are stored lower-cased (e.g. C<'x-mailer'>, not C<'X-Mailer'>).
 2150: Header values are stored verbatim, preserving the original case and
 2151: whitespace.
 2152: 
 2153: =item *
 2154: 
 2155: The C<note> field is a fixed annotation string chosen by the module, not
 2156: text extracted from the message.  It is safe to display directly in reports
 2157: without sanitisation.
 2158: 
 2159: =item *
 2160: 
 2161: If both C<X-PHP-Originating-Script> and C<X-Source> are present (common on
 2162: cPanel systems), both are returned as separate list entries.  A caller
 2163: building a hosting abuse report should include all entries whose C<header>
 2164: begins with C<x->.
 2165: 
 2166: =back
 2167: 
 2168: =head3 API Specification
 2169: 
 2170: =head4 Input
 2171: 
 2172:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 2173:     # No arguments.
 2174:     []
 2175: 
 2176: =head4 Output
 2177: 
 2178:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 2179: 
 2180:     # A list (possibly empty) of hashrefs, in alphabetical header-name order:
 2181:     (
 2182:         {
 2183:             type => HASHREF,
 2184:             keys => {
 2185:                 header => {
 2186:                     type  => SCALAR,
 2187:                     regex => qr/^(?:user-agent|x-mailer|x-php-originating-script
 2188:                                    |x-source|x-source-args|x-source-host)$/x,
 2189:                 },
 2190:                 value => {
 2191:                     type => SCALAR,
 2192:                     # Verbatim header value; may be any non-empty string.
 2193:                 },
 2194:                 note => {
 2195:                     type  => SCALAR,
 2196:                     # Fixed annotation string; one of the six strings
 2197:                     # documented in the Algorithm section above.
 2198:                 },
 2199:             },
 2200:         },
 2201:         # ... one hashref per recognised header present, alphabetical order
 2202:     )
 2203: 
 2204:     # Empty list when none of the six watched headers are present.
 2205: 
 2206: =cut
 2207: 
 2208: sub sending_software {
 2209: 	my $self = $_[0];
 2210: 
 2211: 	return @{ $self->{_sending_sw} };
 2212: }
 2213: 
 2214: 
 2215: =head2 received_trail()
 2216: 
 2217: Returns the per-hop tracking data extracted from the C<Received:> header
 2218: chain: the IP address, envelope recipient address, and server-assigned
 2219: session ID for each relay that handled the message.
 2220: 
 2221: When filing an abuse report with a transit ISP or relay operator, these
 2222: are the identifiers their postmaster team needs to look up the specific
 2223: SMTP session in their mail logs.  Without the session ID or envelope
 2224: recipient, an ISP typically cannot locate a single message among billions
 2225: of log entries; with them, the lookup takes seconds.
 2226: 
 2227: The data is extracted synchronously during C<parse_email()> with no network
 2228: I/O.  This method simply returns the pre-built list.
 2229: 
 2230: =head3 Usage
 2231: 
 2232:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 2233:     my @trail = $analyser->received_trail();
 2234: 
 2235:     for my $hop (@trail) {
 2236:         printf "Hop IP : %s\n",  $hop->{ip}       // '(unknown)';
 2237:         printf "  For  : %s\n",  $hop->{for}       if defined $hop->{for};
 2238:         printf "  ID   : %s\n",  $hop->{id}        if defined $hop->{id};
 2239:         printf "  Raw  : %s\n",  $hop->{received};
 2240:         print  "\n";
 2241:     }
 2242: 
 2243:     # Build a list of session IDs to include in an abuse report
 2244:     my @ids = map  { "$_->{ip}: id $_->{id}" }
 2245:               grep { defined $_->{id} }
 2246:               @trail;
 2247: 
 2248:     # Find which ISP handled a particular relay IP
 2249:     my ($hop) = grep { ($_->{ip} // '') eq '91.198.174.5' } @trail;
 2250:     if ($hop) {
 2251:         print "Session ID at that relay: $hop->{id}\n" if defined $hop->{id};
 2252:     }
 2253: 
 2254: =head3 Arguments
 2255: 
 2256: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 2257: 
 2258: =head3 Returns
 2259: 
 2260: A list (not an arrayref) of hashrefs, one per C<Received:> hop from which
 2261: at least one of an IP address, an envelope recipient address, or a server
 2262: session ID could be extracted, in oldest-first order (i.e. the first element
 2263: is the outermost relay, the last element is the most recent hop before your
 2264: own server).  Returns an empty list if no C<Received:> headers are present
 2265: or none yielded any extractable data, or if C<parse_email()> has not been
 2266: called.
 2267: 
 2268:     (
 2269:       { received => '...raw header...', ip => '1.2.3.4',
 2270:         for => 'victim@example.com', id => 'ABC123' },
 2271:       ...
 2272:     )
 2273: 
 2274: Each hashref contains exactly four keys:
 2275: 
 2276: =over 4
 2277: 
 2278: =item C<received> (string, always present)
 2279: 
 2280: The complete raw value of the C<Received:> header for this hop, exactly as
 2281: it appeared in the message.  Suitable for including verbatim in an abuse
 2282: report so the receiving ISP can see the full context.
 2283: 
 2284: =item C<ip> (string or undef)
 2285: 
 2286: The IPv4 address extracted from this C<Received:> hop, or C<undef> if no
 2287: recognisable IPv4 address was found.  Uses the same four-pattern extraction
 2288: priority as C<originating_ip()>: bracketed C<[1.2.3.4]> first, then
 2289: parenthesised, then C<from hostname addr>, then any bare dotted-quad as a
 2290: last resort.  Private, reserved, and trusted IPs are B<not> filtered here;
 2291: all IPs including RFC 1918 addresses are returned as found.  (Filtering is
 2292: applied only by C<originating_ip()>.)
 2293: 
 2294: =item C<for> (string or undef)
 2295: 
 2296: The envelope recipient address extracted from the C<for> clause of the
 2297: C<Received:> header (e.g. C<for E<lt>victim@example.comE<gt>>), or C<undef>
 2298: if no such clause is present or it does not contain a fully-qualified email
 2299: address (one with both a local part and a domain containing at least one
 2300: dot).  Bare postmaster addresses, C<for multiple recipients>, and similar
 2301: non-address forms are not captured and result in C<undef>.
 2302: 
 2303: =item C<id> (string or undef)
 2304: 
 2305: The server's internal session or queue identifier from the C<id> clause
 2306: of the C<Received:> header (e.g. C<with ESMTP id ABC123XYZ>), or C<undef>
 2307: if no C<id> clause is present.  The value is a single whitespace-delimited
 2308: token of word characters and dots; longer or more structured ID formats may
 2309: be truncated at the first whitespace boundary.
 2310: 
 2311: =back
 2312: 
 2313: =head3 Side Effects
 2314: 
 2315: None.  All data is collected during C<parse_email()> and this method only
 2316: returns the pre-collected list.  No network I/O is performed.
 2317: 
 2318: =head3 Algorithm: extraction and ordering
 2319: 
 2320: During C<parse_email()>, the C<Received:> headers are walked in reverse
 2321: message order (i.e. oldest hop first, which is the same order as
 2322: C<originating_ip()>'s chain walk).  For each header:
 2323: 
 2324: =over 4
 2325: 
 2326: =item 1.
 2327: 
 2328: The IP address is extracted using the same four-pattern priority sequence
 2329: documented in C<originating_ip()>.
 2330: 
 2331: =item 2.
 2332: 
 2333: The envelope recipient is extracted with the pattern
 2334: C<\bfor\s+E<lt>?([^\s>]+@[\w.-]+\.[\w]+)E<gt>?> (case-insensitive).  The
 2335: domain portion of the address must contain at least one dot; single-label
 2336: names such as C<postmaster> are not matched.
 2337: 
 2338: =item 3.
 2339: 
 2340: The session ID is extracted with the pattern C<\bid\s+([\w.-]+)>
 2341: (case-insensitive), capturing the first word-character token following the
 2342: keyword C<id>.
 2343: 
 2344: =item 4.
 2345: 
 2346: If none of the three fields can be extracted (all are C<undef>), the hop is
 2347: silently discarded and does not appear in the result list.  This suppresses
 2348: internal or synthetic hops that carry no useful tracking information.
 2349: 
 2350: =back
 2351: 
 2352: The result list therefore contains only hops that carry at least one
 2353: actionable piece of tracking data.
 2354: 
 2355: =head3 Notes
 2356: 
 2357: =over 4
 2358: 
 2359: =item *
 2360: 
 2361: The result list is reset to empty by each call to C<parse_email()>.  It
 2362: reflects the C<Received:> headers of the current message only.
 2363: 
 2364: =item *
 2365: 
 2366: Oldest-first ordering means C<$trail[0]> is the first relay the message
 2367: passed through after leaving the sender, and C<$trail[-1]> is the last hop
 2368: before your own server.  This is the natural order for walking the chain
 2369: when composing a forwarded abuse report.
 2370: 
 2371: =item *
 2372: 
 2373: C<ip> may be C<undef> for a hop that nonetheless has a valid C<for> or
 2374: C<id> field -- for example, a C<Received:> header added by a local
 2375: delivery agent that does not record an IP.  Always test C<defined
 2376: $hop-E<gt>{ip}> before using it.
 2377: 
 2378: =item *
 2379: 
 2380: C<for> and C<id> are C<undef>, not the empty string, when absent.  C<ip>
 2381: is also C<undef>, not C<'(unknown)'> as in some other methods.  All four
 2382: fields must be tested with C<defined>, not boolean truthiness, to
 2383: distinguish between absent and empty.
 2384: 
 2385: =item *
 2386: 
 2387: C<report()> applies an additional filter when displaying this data: it only
 2388: shows hops where C<id> or C<for> is defined, suppressing hops where only
 2389: an IP was found.  C<received_trail()> itself returns all hops with any
 2390: extractable data, including IP-only hops, giving callers the full picture.
 2391: 
 2392: =item *
 2393: 
 2394: The C<received> field is the unfolded header value as stored after RFC 2822
 2395: line-folding is removed during C<parse_email()>.  Continuation whitespace
 2396: is replaced with a single space; the value will not contain embedded
 2397: newlines.
 2398: 
 2399: =back
 2400: 
 2401: =head3 API Specification
 2402: 
 2403: =head4 Input
 2404: 
 2405:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 2406:     # No arguments.
 2407:     []
 2408: 
 2409: =head4 Output
 2410: 
 2411:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 2412: 
 2413:     # A list (possibly empty) of hashrefs, oldest-hop first:
 2414:     (
 2415:         {
 2416:             type => HASHREF,
 2417:             keys => {
 2418:                 received => {
 2419:                     type => SCALAR,
 2420:                     # Complete unfolded Received: header value; always defined.
 2421:                 },
 2422:                 ip => {
 2423:                     type     => SCALAR,
 2424:                     optional => 1,  # present but may be undef
 2425:                     regex    => qr/^\d{1,3}(?:\.\d{1,3}){3}$/,
 2426:                 },
 2427:                 for => {
 2428:                     type     => SCALAR,
 2429:                     optional => 1,  # present but may be undef
 2430:                     # Fully-qualified email address: local@domain.tld
 2431:                     regex    => qr/^[^\s@]+\@[\w.-]+\.[a-zA-Z]{2,}$/,
 2432:                 },
 2433:                 id => {
 2434:                     type     => SCALAR,
 2435:                     optional => 1,  # present but may be undef
 2436:                     regex    => qr/^[\w.-]+$/,
 2437:                 },
 2438:             },
 2439:         },
 2440:         # ... one hashref per hop with at least one extractable field,
 2441:         #     in oldest-first (outermost relay first) order
 2442:     )
 2443: 
 2444:     # Empty list when no Received: headers are present or none yielded
 2445:     # any extractable data.
 2446: 
 2447: =cut
 2448: 
 2449: sub received_trail {
 2450: 	my $self = $_[0];
 2451: 
 2452: 	return @{ $self->{_rcvd_tracking} };
 2453: }
 2454: 
 2455: =head2 risk_assessment()
 2456: 
 2457: Evaluates the message against a set of heuristic checks and returns an
 2458: overall risk level, a weighted numeric score, and a list of every specific
 2459: red flag that contributed to the score.
 2460: 
 2461: The assessment covers five categories: originating IP characteristics, email
 2462: authentication results, C<Date:> header validity, identity and header
 2463: consistency, and URL and domain properties.  Each finding is assigned a
 2464: severity, a machine-readable flag name, and a human-readable detail string.
 2465: 
 2466: The result is computed once and cached; subsequent calls on the same object
 2467: return the same hashref without repeating any analysis.  Calling
 2468: C<risk_assessment()> also implicitly triggers C<originating_ip()>,
 2469: C<embedded_urls()>, and C<mailto_domains()> if they have not already been
 2470: called, performing all associated network I/O.
 2471: 
 2472: =head3 Usage
 2473: 
 2474:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 2475:     my $risk = $analyser->risk_assessment();
 2476: 
 2477:     printf "Risk level : %s (score: %d)\n", $risk->{level}, $risk->{score};
 2478: 
 2479:     for my $f (@{ $risk->{flags} }) {
 2480:         printf "  [%-6s] %s\n    %s\n",
 2481:             $f->{severity}, $f->{flag}, $f->{detail};
 2482:     }
 2483: 
 2484:     # Gate an automated report on HIGH level only
 2485:     if ($risk->{level} eq 'HIGH') {
 2486:         send_abuse_report($analyser->abuse_report_text());
 2487:     }
 2488: 
 2489:     # Collect only HIGH and MEDIUM flags for a summary
 2490:     my @significant = grep { $_->{severity} =~ /^(?:HIGH|MEDIUM)$/ }
 2491:                       @{ $risk->{flags} };
 2492: 
 2493:     # Check for a specific flag
 2494:     my ($flag) = grep { $_->{flag} eq 'recently_registered_domain' }
 2495:                  @{ $risk->{flags} };
 2496:     warn "Phishing domain suspected\n" if $flag;
 2497: 
 2498:     # INFO level means no actionable red flags
 2499:     if ($risk->{level} eq 'INFO') {
 2500:         print "No significant red flags detected.\n";
 2501:     }
 2502: 
 2503: =head3 Arguments
 2504: 
 2505: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 2506: 
 2507: =head3 Returns
 2508: 
 2509: Returns a hashref with an overall risk level and a list of specific
 2510: red flags found in the message:
 2511: 
 2512:     {
 2513:         level => 'HIGH',          # HIGH | MEDIUM | LOW | INFO
 2514:         score => 7,               # raw weighted score
 2515:         flags => [
 2516:             { severity => 'HIGH',   flag => 'recently_registered_domain',
 2517:               detail => 'firmluminary.com registered 2025-09-01 (< 180 days ago)' },
 2518:             { severity => 'MEDIUM', flag => 'residential_sending_ip',
 2519:               detail => 'rDNS 120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au looks like a broadband line' },
 2520:             { severity => 'MEDIUM', flag => 'url_shortener',
 2521:               detail => 'bit.ly used - real destination hidden' },
 2522:             ...
 2523:         ],
 2524:     }
 2525: 
 2526: A hashref with exactly three keys, all always present:
 2527: 
 2528: =over 4
 2529: 
 2530: =item C<level> (string)
 2531: 
 2532: The overall risk classification, determined by the weighted score:
 2533: 
 2534:     Score >= 9  =>  'HIGH'
 2535:     Score >= 5  =>  'MEDIUM'
 2536:     Score >= 2  =>  'LOW'
 2537:     Score <  2  =>  'INFO'
 2538: 
 2539: C<'INFO'> means either no flags were raised or only zero-weight (INFO
 2540: severity) flags were raised.  It does not mean the message is definitely
 2541: legitimate; it means no significant heuristic evidence of spam was found.
 2542: 
 2543: =item C<score> (integer)
 2544: 
 2545: The sum of the weights of all flags raised.  Weights by severity:
 2546: 
 2547:     HIGH   => 3
 2548:     MEDIUM => 2
 2549:     LOW    => 1
 2550:     INFO   => 0
 2551: 
 2552: The score is a non-negative integer.  Multiple flags of the same severity
 2553: each contribute their full weight independently; there is no cap on the
 2554: score.
 2555: 
 2556: =item C<flags> (arrayref of hashrefs)
 2557: 
 2558: A reference to a list of flag hashrefs, one per red flag raised, in the
 2559: order they were detected.  Each hashref contains exactly three keys:
 2560: 
 2561: =over 8
 2562: 
 2563: =item C<severity> (string)
 2564: 
 2565: One of C<'HIGH'>, C<'MEDIUM'>, C<'LOW'>, or C<'INFO'>.
 2566: 
 2567: =item C<flag> (string)
 2568: 
 2569: A lower-cased, underscore-separated machine-readable identifier.  See the
 2570: Algorithm section for the full list of possible flag names.
 2571: 
 2572: =item C<detail> (string)
 2573: 
 2574: A human-readable sentence describing the specific finding, including the
 2575: values from the message that triggered the flag (domain name, IP address,
 2576: header value, etc.).  Suitable for inclusion in an abuse report or log.
 2577: 
 2578: =back
 2579: 
 2580: The arrayref is empty (C<[]>) when no flags are raised.
 2581: 
 2582: =back
 2583: 
 2584: =head3 Side Effects
 2585: 
 2586: The first call triggers C<originating_ip()>, C<embedded_urls()>, and
 2587: C<mailto_domains()> if they have not already run on the current message.
 2588: Each of those methods may perform network I/O as documented in their own
 2589: entries.  Specifically:
 2590: 
 2591: =over 4
 2592: 
 2593: =item * C<originating_ip()> performs a PTR lookup and RDAP/WHOIS for the
 2594: sending IP.
 2595: 
 2596: =item * C<embedded_urls()> performs an A lookup and RDAP/WHOIS for each
 2597: unique URL hostname.
 2598: 
 2599: =item * C<mailto_domains()> performs A, MX, NS, and WHOIS queries for
 2600: each unique contact domain.
 2601: 
 2602: =back
 2603: 
 2604: All results are cached.  Subsequent calls to C<risk_assessment()> on the
 2605: same object return the cached hashref immediately.  The cache is invalidated
 2606: by C<parse_email()>.
 2607: 
 2608: =head3 Algorithm: flags and scoring
 2609: 
 2610: The following flags may be raised.  They are evaluated in five groups, in
 2611: the order shown.  The same flag name is never raised more than once per
 2612: message.
 2613: 
 2614: B<Group 1 -- Originating IP> (requires C<originating_ip()> to return a
 2615: result):
 2616: 
 2617: =over 4
 2618: 
 2619: =item C<residential_sending_ip> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2620: 
 2621: The rDNS of the sending IP matches patterns associated with residential
 2622: broadband or dynamically-assigned addresses: an embedded dotted-quad, or
 2623: any of the substrings C<dsl>, C<adsl>, C<cable>, C<broad>, C<dial>,
 2624: C<dynamic>, C<dhcp>, C<ppp>, C<residential>, C<cust>, C<home>, C<pool>,
 2625: C<client>, C<user>, C<staticN>, or C<hostN>.
 2626: 
 2627: =item C<no_reverse_dns> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2628: 
 2629: The sending IP has no PTR record, or the PTR lookup returned the sentinel
 2630: C<'(no reverse DNS)'>.  Legitimate mail servers invariably have rDNS.
 2631: 
 2632: =item C<low_confidence_origin> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2633: 
 2634: The originating IP was taken from an unverified header (C<X-Originating-IP>)
 2635: rather than from the C<Received:> chain.  Confidence level is C<'low'>.
 2636: 
 2637: =item C<high_spam_country> (INFO, weight 0)
 2638: 
 2639: The sending IP's country code is one of: C<CN> (China), C<RU> (Russia),
 2640: C<NG> (Nigeria), C<VN> (Vietnam), C<IN> (India), C<PK> (Pakistan),
 2641: C<BD> (Bangladesh).  Informational only; does not contribute to the score.
 2642: 
 2643: =back
 2644: 
 2645: B<Group 2 -- Email authentication> (from C<Authentication-Results:> header):
 2646: 
 2647: =over 4
 2648: 
 2649: =item C<spf_fail> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2650: 
 2651: SPF result is C<fail>, C<permerror>, C<temperror>, C<none>, or any value
 2652: other than C<pass> or C<softfail>.  The sending IP is not authorised by
 2653: the domain's SPF record.
 2654: 
 2655: =item C<spf_softfail> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2656: 
 2657: SPF result is C<softfail> (C<~all>).  The sending IP is not explicitly
 2658: authorised but the domain policy does not hard-fail it.
 2659: 
 2660: =item C<dkim_fail> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2661: 
 2662: DKIM result is present and is any value other than C<pass>.
 2663: 
 2664: =item C<dmarc_fail> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2665: 
 2666: DMARC result is present and is any value other than C<pass>.
 2667: 
 2668: =item C<dkim_domain_mismatch> (INFO or MEDIUM, weight 0 or 2)
 2669: 
 2670: The DKIM signing domain (C<d=> tag) differs from the registrable domain
 2671: of the C<From:> address.  Raised at INFO (weight 0) when DKIM passes --
 2672: this is normal for bulk senders using ESPs such as SendGrid or Mailchimp.
 2673: Raised at MEDIUM (weight 2) when DKIM fails or is absent -- a differing
 2674: domain combined with a failed signature is more suspicious.
 2675: 
 2676: =back
 2677: 
 2678: B<Group 3 -- Date: header>:
 2679: 
 2680: =over 4
 2681: 
 2682: =item C<missing_date> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2683: 
 2684: No C<Date:> header is present, or it contains only whitespace.  Violates
 2685: RFC 5322; common in programmatically-generated spam.
 2686: 
 2687: =item C<suspicious_date> (LOW, weight 1)
 2688: 
 2689: The C<Date:> header is present but more than 7 days in the past or more
 2690: than 7 days in the future relative to the time of analysis.  Timezone
 2691: offsets are ignored during comparison (maximum error: approximately 14
 2692: hours, well within the 7-day window).
 2693: 
 2694: =back
 2695: 
 2696: B<Group 4 -- Header identity and consistency>:
 2697: 
 2698: =over 4
 2699: 
 2700: =item C<display_name_domain_spoof> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2701: 
 2702: The C<From:> display name contains a domain name (matched against the
 2703: suffixes C<.com>, C<.net>, C<.org>, C<.io>, C<.co>, C<.uk>, C<.au>,
 2704: C<.gov>, C<.edu>) that differs at the registrable level from the actual
 2705: C<From:> address domain.  Example: C<"PayPal paypal.com" E<lt>phish@evil.exampleE<gt>>.
 2706: 
 2707: =item C<free_webmail_sender> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2708: 
 2709: The C<From:> address belongs to a free webmail provider: Gmail, Yahoo,
 2710: Hotmail, Outlook, Live, AOL, ProtonMail, Yandex, or mail.ru.
 2711: 
 2712: =item C<reply_to_differs_from_from> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2713: 
 2714: A C<Reply-To:> header is present and its email address differs from the
 2715: C<From:> address (case-insensitive comparison).  Replies will be harvested
 2716: by a different address than the apparent sender.
 2717: 
 2718: =item C<undisclosed_recipients> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2719: 
 2720: The C<To:> header is absent, empty, contains the string C<undisclosed>, or
 2721: matches the group-syntax sentinel C<:;>.
 2722: 
 2723: =item C<encoded_subject> (LOW, weight 1)
 2724: 
 2725: The C<Subject:> header contains a MIME encoded-word sequence
 2726: (C<=?charset?encoding?text?=>).  Often used to evade keyword filters.
 2727: 
 2728: =back
 2729: 
 2730: B<Group 5 -- URLs and domains> (from C<embedded_urls()> and
 2731: C<mailto_domains()>):
 2732: 
 2733: =over 4
 2734: 
 2735: =item C<url_shortener> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2736: 
 2737: At least one URL hostname is in the built-in URL shortener list (over 25
 2738: services including C<bit.ly>, C<tinyurl.com>, C<t.co>, C<ow.ly>, etc.).
 2739: Raised at most once per unique shortener hostname per message.
 2740: 
 2741: =item C<http_not_https> (LOW, weight 1)
 2742: 
 2743: At least one URL uses the plain C<http://> scheme rather than C<https://>.
 2744: Raised at most once per unique hostname.
 2745: 
 2746: =item C<recently_registered_domain> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2747: 
 2748: At least one contact domain was registered less than 180 days before the
 2749: time of analysis.
 2750: 
 2751: =item C<domain_expires_soon> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2752: 
 2753: At least one contact domain expires within the next 30 days.  Suggests a
 2754: throwaway domain.
 2755: 
 2756: =item C<domain_expired> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2757: 
 2758: At least one contact domain has already passed its expiry date.
 2759: 
 2760: =item C<lookalike_domain> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2761: 
 2762: At least one contact domain contains the name of a well-known brand
 2763: (C<paypal>, C<apple>, C<google>, C<amazon>, C<microsoft>, C<netflix>,
 2764: C<ebay>, C<instagram>, C<facebook>, C<twitter>, C<linkedin>,
 2765: C<bankofamerica>, C<wellsfargo>, C<chase>, C<barclays>, C<hsbc>,
 2766: C<lloyds>, C<santander>) but is not the brand's own canonical domain
 2767: (e.g. C<paypal.com>, C<paypal.co.uk>).
 2768: 
 2769: =back
 2770: 
 2771: =head3 Notes
 2772: 
 2773: =over 4
 2774: 
 2775: =item *
 2776: 
 2777: The C<flags> arrayref is a reference to the module's internal list.
 2778: Callers must not modify it.  To iterate safely, use C<@{ $risk-E<gt>{flags} }>.
 2779: 
 2780: =item *
 2781: 
 2782: Flags are not deduplicated across categories.  If C<spf_fail> and
 2783: C<dkim_fail> both apply, both appear in the list and both contribute to
 2784: the score.
 2785: 
 2786: =item *
 2787: 
 2788: C<high_spam_country> and C<dkim_domain_mismatch> (when DKIM passes)
 2789: contribute zero to the score.  Their presence does not change the level
 2790: classification, but they appear in the C<flags> list so callers can
 2791: include them in reports.
 2792: 
 2793: =item *
 2794: 
 2795: The level thresholds are fixed constants: HIGH >= 9, MEDIUM >= 5, LOW >= 2,
 2796: INFO < 2.  They are not configurable.
 2797: 
 2798: =item *
 2799: 
 2800: C<risk_assessment()> does not directly raise flags for domains found only
 2801: in URLs (C<embedded_urls()> hosts); domain checks in Group 5 apply only
 2802: to domains from C<mailto_domains()>.  URL hostname checks (shorteners,
 2803: HTTP) use the C<embedded_urls()> list.
 2804: 
 2805: =item *
 2806: 
 2807: If C<parse_email()> has not been called, or was called with an empty or
 2808: malformed message, C<risk_assessment()> returns a valid hashref with
 2809: C<level =E<gt> 'INFO'>, C<score =E<gt> 0>, and C<flags =E<gt> []>.
 2810: 
 2811: =back
 2812: 
 2813: =head3 API Specification
 2814: 
 2815: =head4 Input
 2816: 
 2817:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 2818:     # No arguments.
 2819:     []
 2820: 
 2821: =head4 Output
 2822: 
 2823:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 2824:     {
 2825:         type => HASHREF,
 2826:         keys => {
 2827:             level => {
 2828:                 type  => SCALAR,
 2829:                 regex => qr/^(?:HIGH|MEDIUM|LOW|INFO)$/,
 2830:             },
 2831:             score => {
 2832:                 type  => SCALAR,
 2833:                 regex => qr/^\d+$/,  # non-negative integer
 2834:             },
 2835:             flags => {
 2836:                 type => ARRAYREF,
 2837:                 # Reference to a list (possibly empty) of hashrefs:
 2838:                 # [
 2839:                 #   {
 2840:                 #     severity => qr/^(?:HIGH|MEDIUM|LOW|INFO)$/,
 2841:                 #     flag     => qr/^[a-z][a-z0-9_]+$/,
 2842:                 #     detail   => SCALAR,  # human-readable string
 2843:                 #   },
 2844:                 #   ...
 2845:                 # ]
 2846:             },
 2847:         },
 2848:     }
 2849: 
 2850: =cut
 2851:

 3110: 
 3111: =head2 abuse_report_text()
 3112: 
 3113: Produces a compact, plain-text string intended to be sent as the body of
 3114: an abuse report email to an ISP or hosting provider.  It summarises the
 3115: risk level, lists every red flag with its detail, identifies the originating
 3116: IP and its network owner, lists the abuse contacts, and appends the complete
 3117: message headers so the recipient can trace the session in their own logs.
 3118: 
 3119: The message body is intentionally omitted to keep the report concise.
 3120: Headers are sufficient for an ISP to locate the relevant mail session; the
 3121: body adds bulk without aiding the investigation.
 3122: 
 3123: This method is the companion to C<abuse_contacts()>: call
 3124: C<abuse_contacts()> to obtain the addresses to send the report to, and
 3125: C<abuse_report_text()> to obtain the text to send.  Use C<report()> instead
 3126: when you want a comprehensive analyst-facing document rather than a
 3127: send-ready ISP report.
 3128: 
 3129: =head3 Usage
 3130: 
 3131:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 3132: 
 3133:     my $text     = $analyser->abuse_report_text();
 3134:     my @contacts = $analyser->abuse_contacts();
 3135: 
 3136:     for my $c (@contacts) {
 3137:         send_email(
 3138:             to      => $c->{address},
 3139:             subject => 'Abuse report: ' . ($analyser->originating_ip()->{ip} // 'unknown'),
 3140:             body    => $text,
 3141:         );
 3142:     }
 3143: 
 3144:     # Print to stdout for manual review before sending
 3145:     print $text;
 3146: 
 3147:     # Write to file for a ticketing system
 3148:     open my $fh, '>', 'abuse_report.txt' or die $!;
 3149:     print $fh $text;
 3150:     close $fh;
 3151: 
 3152: =head3 Arguments
 3153: 
 3154: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 3155: 
 3156: =head3 Returns
 3157: 
 3158: A plain scalar string containing the report text.  The string is
 3159: newline-terminated and uses Unix line endings (C<\n>) throughout.
 3160: The string is never empty; it always contains at least the boilerplate
 3161: introduction and the risk-level line, even if no red flags were found.
 3162: 
 3163: The report is structured as follows, in order:
 3164: 
 3165: =over 4
 3166: 
 3167: =item 1. Introduction
 3168: 
 3169: Two fixed lines:
 3170: 
 3171:     This is an automated abuse report generated by Email::Abuse::Investigator.
 3172:     Please investigate the following spam/phishing message.
 3173: 
 3174: =item 2. Risk level
 3175: 
 3176:     RISK LEVEL: HIGH (score: 11)
 3177: 
 3178: =item 3. Red flags (omitted if no flags were raised)
 3179: 
 3180:     RED FLAGS IDENTIFIED:
 3181:       [HIGH] firmluminary.com was registered 2025-09-01 (less than 180 days ago)
 3182:       [MEDIUM] rDNS 120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au looks like a broadband line
 3183:       ...
 3184: 
 3185: Each flag is formatted as C<[SEVERITY] detail-string>, one per line,
 3186: indented two spaces.  The flag machine-name is not included; only the
 3187: human-readable detail string is shown, matching what a postmaster would
 3188: want to read.
 3189: 
 3190: =item 4. Originating IP (omitted if C<originating_ip()> returns C<undef>)
 3191: 
 3192:     ORIGINATING IP: 120.88.161.249 (120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au)
 3193:     NETWORK OWNER:  TPG Telecom Limited
 3194: 
 3195: =item 5. Abuse contacts (omitted if C<abuse_contacts()> returns an empty list)
 3196: 
 3197:     ABUSE CONTACTS:
 3198:       abuse@tpg.com.au (Sending ISP)
 3199:       abuse@registrar.example (Domain registrar for firmluminary.com)
 3200: 
 3201: =item 6. Original message headers
 3202: 
 3203:     ------------------------------------------------------------------------
 3204:     ORIGINAL MESSAGE HEADERS:
 3205:     ------------------------------------------------------------------------
 3206:     received: from 120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au ...
 3207:     from: Sender <spammer@firmluminary.com>
 3208:     ...
 3209: 
 3210: All parsed headers are emitted, one per line, in the order they appeared
 3211: in the original message.  Header names are lower-cased (as normalised
 3212: during C<parse_email()>).  Header values are verbatim.  The message
 3213: body is not included.
 3214: 
 3215: =back
 3216: 
 3217: =head3 Side Effects
 3218: 
 3219: Calls C<risk_assessment()>, C<originating_ip()>, and C<abuse_contacts()>
 3220: if they have not already run, which in turn may perform network I/O as
 3221: documented in those methods.  All results are cached; the text is not
 3222: itself cached, but re-computing it is cheap since all the underlying data
 3223: is already cached.
 3224: 
 3225: =head3 Notes
 3226: 
 3227: =over 4
 3228: 
 3229: =item *
 3230: 
 3231: Header names in the output are lower-cased (e.g. C<from:>, C<received:>),
 3232: because that is how they are stored internally after C<parse_email()>
 3233: normalises them.  Postmasters are accustomed to receiving headers in their
 3234: original mixed case; if canonical capitalisation is required, a simple
 3235: substitution (C<s/^([\w-]+)/\u\L$1/>) will restore it.
 3236: 
 3237: =item *
 3238: 
 3239: The message body is deliberately excluded.  This avoids transmitting
 3240: potentially malicious or offensive content to third parties, keeps the
 3241: report below common size limits for abuse mailboxes, and is consistent
 3242: with the RFC 2646 / ARF (Abuse Reporting Format) practice of including
 3243: only the headers in a first-contact report.  To include the body, callers
 3244: can append C<$self-E<gt>{_raw}> directly, though this is not recommended.
 3245: 
 3246: =item *
 3247: 
 3248: The separator lines are exactly 72 hyphens (C<-> x 72), matching the
 3249: separator width used by C<report()>.
 3250: 
 3251: =item *
 3252: 
 3253: The output is suitable for use as a plain-text email body.  It is not
 3254: ARF (RFC 5965) compliant; it does not include a C<message/feedback-report>
 3255: MIME part.  For ARF-compliant reporting, use the output of this method as
 3256: the human-readable first part and add the ARF metadata separately.
 3257: 
 3258: =item *
 3259: 
 3260: If C<parse_email()> has not been called, all sections that depend on
 3261: analysis will be empty (no flags, no originating IP, no contacts) and the
 3262: header section will be blank.  The method will not die.
 3263: 
 3264: =back
 3265: 
 3266: =head3 API Specification
 3267: 
 3268: =head4 Input
 3269: 
 3270:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 3271:     # No arguments.
 3272:     []
 3273: 
 3274: =head4 Output
 3275: 
 3276:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 3277:     {
 3278:         type  => SCALAR,
 3279:         # Non-empty plain-text string, newline-terminated.
 3280:         # Always defined; never undef.
 3281:         # Line endings: Unix LF (\n) only.
 3282:         # Minimum content: introduction + risk-level line.
 3283:     }
 3284: 
 3285: =cut
 3286:

 3340: 	for my $h (@{ $self->{_headers} }) {
 3341: 		push @out, "$h->{name}: $h->{value}";
 3342: 	}
●3343 → 3345 → 0 3343: 	push @out, '';
 3344: 
 3345: 	return join("\n", @out);
 3346: }
 3347: 
 3348: =head2 abuse_contacts()
 3349: 
 3350: Collates the complete set of parties that should receive an abuse report
 3351: for this message: the ISP that owns the sending IP, the operators of every
 3352: URL host, the web, mail, and DNS hosts of every contact domain, each
 3353: domain's registrar, the webmail or ESP account provider identified from
 3354: key headers, the DKIM signing organisation, and the ESP identified via
 3355: the C<List-Unsubscribe:> header.
 3356: 
 3357: For each party the method produces the role description, the abuse email
 3358: address, a supporting note, and the source of the information.  Addresses
 3359: are deduplicated globally: if the same address is discovered through
 3360: multiple routes (e.g. Google as both the sending ISP and the owner of a
 3361: blogspot.com URL in the message body), it appears only once.  The C<role>
 3362: string for that entry is the combined description of all routes that found
 3363: it, joined by C<" and ">, and the C<roles> key holds the individual role
 3364: strings as an arrayref.
 3365: 
 3366: This method is designed to be used together with C<abuse_report_text()>:
 3367: iterate over the returned contacts to obtain the list of addresses, and
 3368: send the text from C<abuse_report_text()> to each one.
 3369: 
 3370: =head3 Usage
 3371: 
 3372:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 3373:     my @contacts = $analyser->abuse_contacts();
 3374: 
 3375:     for my $c (@contacts) {
 3376:         printf "Role    : %s\n", $c->{role};
 3377:         printf "Send to : %s\n", $c->{address};
 3378:         printf "Note    : %s\n", $c->{note}  if $c->{note};
 3379:         printf "Source  : %s\n", $c->{via};
 3380:         print  "\n";
 3381:     }
 3382: 
 3383:     # Collect addresses for sending
 3384:     my @addresses = map { $_->{address} } @contacts;
 3385: 
 3386:     # Filter to WHOIS-discovered contacts only
 3387:     my @whois_contacts = grep { $_->{via} =~ /whois/ } @contacts;
 3388: 
 3389:     # Check whether any registrar abuse contacts were found
 3390:     my @registrar = grep { $_->{role} =~ /registrar/ } @contacts;
 3391: 
 3392: =head3 Arguments
 3393: 
 3394: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 3395: 
 3396: =head3 Returns
 3397: 
 3398: A list (not an arrayref) of hashrefs, one per unique abuse contact address,
 3399: in the order they were first discovered.  Returns an empty list if no
 3400: actionable abuse contacts can be determined, or if C<parse_email()> has
 3401: not been called.
 3402: 
 3403: Returns a de-duplicated list of hashrefs, one per party that should
 3404: receive an abuse report, in priority order:
 3405: 
 3406:     {
 3407:         role    => 'Sending ISP',          # human-readable role
 3408:         address => 'abuse@senderisp.example',
 3409:         note    => 'IP block 120.88.0.0/14 owner',
 3410:         via     => 'ip-whois',             # ip-whois | domain-whois | provider-table | rdap
 3411:     }
 3412: 
 3413: Roles produced (in order):
 3414: 
 3415:   Sending ISP            - network owner of the originating IP
 3416:   URL host               - network owner of each unique web-server IP
 3417:   Mail host (MX)         - network owner of the domain's MX record IP
 3418:   DNS host (NS)          - network owner of the authoritative NS IP
 3419:   Domain registrar       - registrar abuse contact from domain WHOIS
 3420:   Account provider       - e.g. Gmail / Outlook for the From:/Sender: account
 3421:   DKIM signer            - the organisation whose key signed the message
 3422:   ESP / bulk sender      - identified via List-Unsubscribe: domain
 3423: 
 3424: Addresses are deduplicated so the same address never appears twice,
 3425: even if it is discovered through multiple routes.
 3426: 
 3427: Each hashref contains the following keys, all always present:
 3428: 
 3429: =over 4
 3430: 
 3431: =item C<role> (string)
 3432: 
 3433: A human-readable description of the party's relationship to the message.
 3434: When the same address was found via multiple discovery routes, the role
 3435: strings from each route are joined with C<" and "> (e.g.
 3436: C<"Sending ISP (provider table) and URL host (provider table)">).
 3437: See the Algorithm section for the full set of role string patterns.
 3438: 
 3439: =item C<roles> (arrayref of strings)
 3440: 
 3441: The individual role strings for each discovery route that found this
 3442: address, in discovery order.  Contains exactly one element when the
 3443: address was found via a single route; two or more elements when multiple
 3444: routes converged on the same address.  The C<role> key is always the
 3445: C<join(' and ', @{$c->{roles}})> of this arrayref.
 3446: 
 3447: =item C<address> (string)
 3448: 
 3449: The abuse contact email address, lower-cased.  Always contains an C<@>
 3450: sign.  Deduplicated globally: each distinct address appears at most once
 3451: across the entire list, regardless of how many discovery routes found it.
 3452: 
 3453: =item C<note> (string)
 3454: 
 3455: Supporting information about why this party was identified and what action
 3456: to request.  For provider-table entries this is the note from the built-in
 3457: table (which may include a URL to a web-based abuse form).  For WHOIS- and
 3458: RDAP-discovered entries this describes the IP block or domain involved.
 3459: Always defined; may be the empty string for entries where no note is
 3460: available.  When roles are merged, this reflects the note from the first
 3461: discovery route.
 3462: 
 3463: =item C<via> (string)
 3464: 
 3465: The discovery method for the first route that found this address.  One of:
 3466: 
 3467: =over 8
 3468: 
 3469: =item C<'provider-table'>
 3470: 
 3471: The address was found in the module's built-in table of well-known
 3472: providers (Google, Microsoft, Cloudflare, SendGrid, Mailchimp, etc.).
 3473: Provider-table addresses take priority over WHOIS for the same entity
 3474: because they are curated and point to the right team, whereas generic
 3475: WHOIS contacts sometimes route to NOCs rather than abuse desks.
 3476: 
 3477: =item C<'ip-whois'>
 3478: 
 3479: The address was obtained from an RDAP or WHOIS lookup on an IP block
 3480: (the sending IP, a URL host IP, or an MX/NS IP).
 3481: 
 3482: =item C<'domain-whois'>
 3483: 
 3484: The address was obtained from a WHOIS lookup on a domain name (registrar
 3485: abuse contact from the C<Registrar Abuse Contact Email:> or equivalent
 3486: field).
 3487: 
 3488: =back
 3489: 
 3490: =back
 3491: 
 3492: =head3 Side Effects
 3493: 
 3494: Triggers C<originating_ip()>, C<embedded_urls()>, and C<mailto_domains()>
 3495: if they have not already run on the current message, performing all
 3496: associated network I/O as documented in those methods.  Additionally
 3497: consults the built-in provider table and the cached authentication results;
 3498: neither requires network I/O.
 3499: 
 3500: The result is not independently cached.  Each call recomputes the contact
 3501: list from the cached results of the underlying methods.  Because those
 3502: results are cached, subsequent calls are fast (no network I/O), but they
 3503: do re-execute the collation and deduplication logic.
 3504: 
 3505: =head3 Algorithm: discovery routes
 3506: 
 3507: Contacts are discovered through six routes, applied in order.
 3508: Deduplication is global across all routes: if an address is found by
 3509: more than one route, a single entry is kept and the role strings from
 3510: every route that found it are accumulated into C<roles> and joined into
 3511: C<role>.  An entry is suppressed entirely if its address is empty, does
 3512: not contain an C<@> sign, or is the sentinel C<'(unknown)'>.
 3513: 
 3514: =over 4
 3515: 
 3516: =item Route 1 -- Sending ISP
 3517: 
 3518: The originating IP from C<originating_ip()> is looked up in the built-in
 3519: provider table (by rDNS hostname, stripping subdomains until a match is
 3520: found).  If found, a C<provider-table> entry is added with role
 3521: C<"Sending ISP (provider table)">.
 3522: 
 3523: The C<abuse> field from C<originating_ip()> (obtained from RDAP/WHOIS) is
 3524: then added as an C<ip-whois> entry with role C<"Sending ISP">, unless it
 3525: is C<'(unknown)'>.
 3526: 
 3527: =item Route 2 -- URL hosts
 3528: 
 3529: For each unique hostname in C<embedded_urls()>, the built-in provider
 3530: table is consulted (by hostname, stripping subdomains).  If found, a
 3531: C<provider-table> entry is added with role C<"URL host (provider table)">.
 3532: 
 3533: The C<abuse> field from the URL hashref is then added as an C<ip-whois>
 3534: entry with role C<"URL host">, unless it is C<'(unknown)'>.
 3535: 
 3536: Each unique hostname is processed at most once; multiple URLs on the same
 3537: host do not generate multiple contacts.
 3538: 
 3539: =item Route 3 -- Contact domain hosting and registration
 3540: 
 3541: For each domain from C<mailto_domains()>, up to four contacts may be
 3542: generated:
 3543: 
 3544: =over 8
 3545: 
 3546: =item *
 3547: 
 3548: B<Web host>: if C<web_abuse> is present, both a provider-table lookup
 3549: on the domain name and the WHOIS-derived C<web_abuse> address are tried.
 3550: Role: C<"Web host of $domain"> or C<"Web host of $domain (provider table)">.
 3551: 
 3552: =item *
 3553: 
 3554: B<Mail host (MX)>: if C<mx_abuse> is present.
 3555: Role: C<"Mail host (MX) for $domain">, via C<ip-whois>.
 3556: 
 3557: =item *
 3558: 
 3559: B<DNS host (NS)>: if C<ns_abuse> is present.
 3560: Role: C<"DNS host (NS) for $domain">, via C<ip-whois>.
 3561: 
 3562: =item *
 3563: 
 3564: B<Domain registrar>: if C<registrar_abuse> is present.
 3565: Role: C<"Domain registrar for $domain">, via C<domain-whois>.
 3566: 
 3567: =back
 3568: 
 3569: =item Route 4 -- Account provider
 3570: 
 3571: The C<From:>, C<Reply-To:>, C<Return-Path:>, and C<Sender:> header values
 3572: are inspected in that order.  The domain portion of each address is looked
 3573: up in the built-in provider table (stripping subdomains until a match).
 3574: If found, a C<provider-table> entry is added with role
 3575: C<"Account provider ($header: $value)">.  This identifies the webmail
 3576: or ESP service that hosts the sender's account.
 3577: 
 3578: =item Route 5 -- DKIM signing organisation
 3579: 
 3580: The C<d=> tag from the C<DKIM-Signature:> header is looked up in the
 3581: built-in provider table.  If found, a C<provider-table> entry is added
 3582: with role C<"DKIM signer (provider table): $domain">.  The full domain
 3583: pipeline (web/MX/NS/WHOIS) for this domain is already handled via Route 3
 3584: through C<mailto_domains()>.
 3585: 
 3586: =item Route 6 -- ESP / bulk sender (List-Unsubscribe)
 3587: 
 3588: Both C<https://> URLs and C<mailto:> addresses in the C<List-Unsubscribe:>
 3589: header are parsed for their domains.  Each unique domain is looked up in
 3590: the built-in provider table.  If found, a C<provider-table> entry is added
 3591: with role C<"ESP / bulk sender (List-Unsubscribe: $domain)">.
 3592: 
 3593: =back
 3594: 
 3595: =head3 Notes
 3596: 
 3597: =over 4
 3598: 
 3599: =item *
 3600: 
 3601: Deduplication is by lower-cased address only.  Two contacts with different
 3602: roles but the same address result in a single entry using the data from
 3603: whichever route found it first.  The later route's role and note are
 3604: silently discarded.
 3605: 
 3606: =item *
 3607: 
 3608: The provider table contains curated entries for approximately 50
 3609: well-known domains including major webmail providers (Gmail, Outlook,
 3610: Yahoo, Apple), CDNs and hosters (Cloudflare, Fastly, Akamai, AWS,
 3611: DigitalOcean, Vultr, Hetzner, Contabo, Leaseweb, M247, OVH, Linode),
 3612: ESPs (SendGrid, Mailchimp, Mailgun, Postmark, Brevo, Klaviyo, Campaign
 3613: Monitor, Constant Contact, HubSpot), registrars (GoDaddy, Namecheap),
 3614: and ISPs (TPG, Internode).  Subdomain matching strips labels left-to-right
 3615: until a match is found, so C<mail.sendgrid.net> matches C<sendgrid.net>.
 3616: 
 3617: =item *
 3618: 
 3619: Provider-table entries take priority in the sense that they are added
 3620: first; if the WHOIS address happens to match the provider-table address,
 3621: the WHOIS entry is suppressed by deduplication.  If they differ (unusual
 3622: but possible), both are added.
 3623: 
 3624: =item *
 3625: 
 3626: The result is not cached.  If you call C<abuse_contacts()> multiple times
 3627: on the same object, the full collation runs each time.  If this is a
 3628: concern, store the result in a variable:
 3629: C<my @contacts = $analyser-E<gt>abuse_contacts()>.
 3630: 
 3631: =item *
 3632: 
 3633: An empty list is returned if the message has no usable originating IP, no
 3634: extractable URLs, no contact domains, and no recognised provider-table
 3635: matches.  This is unusual in practice but can occur for very sparse or
 3636: malformed messages.
 3637: 
 3638: =back
 3639: 
 3640: =head3 API Specification
 3641: 
 3642: =head4 Input
 3643: 
 3644:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 3645:     # No arguments.
 3646:     []
 3647: 
 3648: =head4 Output
 3649: 
 3650:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 3651: 
 3652:     # A list (possibly empty) of hashrefs, in discovery order:
 3653:     (
 3654:         {
 3655:             type => HASHREF,
 3656:             keys => {
 3657:                 role => {
 3658:                     type => SCALAR,
 3659:                     # Human-readable role description; always defined,
 3660:                     # may contain inline domain/IP/header values.
 3661:                 },
 3662:                 address => {
 3663:                     type  => SCALAR,
 3664:                     regex => qr/^[^\s@]+\@[^\s@]+$/,
 3665:                     # Lower-cased email address; unique across the list.
 3666:                 },
 3667:                 note => {
 3668:                     type => SCALAR,
 3669:                     # Supporting detail; always defined, may be empty string.
 3670:                 },
 3671:                 via => {
 3672:                     type  => SCALAR,
 3673:                     regex => qr/^(?:provider-table|ip-whois|domain-whois)$/,
 3674:                 },
 3675:             },
 3676:         },
 3677:         # ... one hashref per unique address, in first-discovered order
 3678:     )
 3679: 
 3680:     # Empty list when no actionable abuse contacts can be determined.
 3681: 
 3682: =cut

 3963: =head2 form_contacts()
 3964: 
 3965: Returns the list of parties that require abuse reports to be submitted via
 3966: a web form rather than (or in addition to) email.  These are providers whose
 3967: C<%PROVIDER_ABUSE> entry has a C<form> key.  Each hashref contains the form
 3968: URL, instructions on what to paste, instructions on what to upload (if
 3969: applicable), the role, and the discovery note.
 3970: 
 3971: Returns a list (not an arrayref) of hashrefs, one per unique form contact,
 3972: in discovery order.  Returns an empty list if no form-only or form-plus-email
 3973: contacts are found.
 3974: 
 3975: Each hashref contains:
 3976: 
 3977: =over 4
 3978: 
 3979: =item C<form> (string) -- the URL of the web form
 3980: 
 3981: =item C<role> (string) -- human-readable role, same format as C<abuse_contacts()>
 3982: 
 3983: =item C<note> (string) -- supporting detail about the provider
 3984: 
 3985: =item C<form_paste> (string, optional) -- what text to paste into the form
 3986: 
 3987: =item C<form_upload> (string, optional) -- what file to attach to the form
 3988: 
 3989: =item C<via> (string) -- always C<'provider-table'>
 3990: 
 3991: =back
 3992: 
 3993: =cut
 3994:

 4142: 
 4143: =head2 report()
 4144: 
 4145: Returns a formatted plain-text abuse report.
 4146: 
 4147: Produces a comprehensive, analyst-facing plain-text report covering all
 4148: findings from every analysis method.  It is the single-document summary
 4149: of everything the module knows about a message: envelope fields, risk
 4150: assessment, originating host, sending software, received chain tracking
 4151: IDs, embedded URLs grouped by hostname, contact domain intelligence, and
 4152: the recommended abuse contacts.
 4153: 
 4154: Use C<report()> when you want a human-readable document for review,
 4155: logging, or a ticketing system.  Use C<abuse_report_text()> when you want
 4156: a compact string to transmit to an ISP abuse desk.
 4157: 
 4158: =head3 Usage
 4159: 
 4160:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 4161:     my $text = $analyser->report();
 4162:     print $text;
 4163: 
 4164:     # Write to a file
 4165:     open my $fh, '>', 'report.txt' or die $!;
 4166:     print $fh $analyser->report();
 4167:     close $fh;
 4168: 
 4169:     # Log the risk level line from the report
 4170:     my ($level_line) = $analyser->report() =~ /(\[ RISK ASSESSMENT: [^\]]+\])/;
 4171:     $logger->info($level_line);
 4172: 
 4173:     # Check idempotency -- safe to call multiple times
 4174:     my $r1 = $analyser->report();
 4175:     my $r2 = $analyser->report();
 4176:     # $r1 eq $r2 is always true for the same parsed message
 4177: 
 4178: =head3 Arguments
 4179: 
 4180: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 4181: 
 4182: =head3 Returns
 4183: 
 4184: A plain scalar string containing the full report, newline-terminated,
 4185: using Unix line endings (C<\n>) throughout.  The string is never empty;
 4186: it always contains at least the header banner and envelope summary section.
 4187: 
 4188: The report is structured as nine sections separated by blank lines, in
 4189: this fixed order:
 4190: 
 4191: =over 4
 4192: 
 4193: =item 1. Banner
 4194: 
 4195:       Email::Abuse::Investigator Report  (vX.XX)
 4196: 
 4197: A row of 72 equals signs, the module name and version number, and a
 4198: closing row of 72 equals signs.
 4199: 
 4200: =item 2. Envelope summary
 4201: 
 4202: Up to six header fields, each decoded from MIME encoded-words where
 4203: applicable.  If a field was encoded, the decoded form is shown first
 4204: followed by the raw encoded original in brackets:
 4205: 
 4206:     From           : PayPal Security <phish@evil.example>
 4207:     Reply-to       : Replies <harvest@collector.example>
 4208:     Return-path    : <phish@evil.example>
 4209:     Subject        : Account Alert  [encoded: =?UTF-8?B?QWNjb3VudA==?=]
 4210:     Date           : Mon, 01 Jan 2024 00:00:00 +0000
 4211:     Message-id     : <msg001@evil.example>
 4212: 
 4213: Fields examined (in order): C<From:>, C<Reply-To:>, C<Return-Path:>,
 4214: C<Subject:>, C<Date:>, C<Message-ID:>.  Fields not present in the message
 4215: are silently omitted.
 4216: 
 4217: =item 3. Risk assessment
 4218: 
 4219:     [ RISK ASSESSMENT: HIGH (score: 11) ]
 4220:       [HIGH] firmluminary.com was registered 2025-09-01 (less than 180 days ago)
 4221:       [MEDIUM] rDNS 120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au looks like a broadband/residential line
 4222:       ...
 4223: 
 4224: Or, when no flags were raised:
 4225: 
 4226:     [ RISK ASSESSMENT: INFO (score: 0) ]
 4227:       (no specific red flags detected)
 4228: 
 4229: Each flag is shown as C<[SEVERITY] detail-string>.
 4230: 
 4231: =item 4. Originating host
 4232: 
 4233:     [ ORIGINATING HOST ]
 4234:       IP           : 120.88.161.249
 4235:       Reverse DNS  : 120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au
 4236:       Country      : AU
 4237:       Organisation : TPG Telecom Limited
 4238:       Abuse addr   : abuse@tpg.com.au
 4239:       Confidence   : high
 4240:       Note         : First external hop in Received: chain
 4241: 
 4242: Or C<(could not determine originating IP)> if C<originating_ip()> returns
 4243: C<undef>.  Fields with no value are omitted.
 4244: 
 4245: =item 5. Sending software (omitted entirely if no software headers found)
 4246: 
 4247:     [ SENDING SOFTWARE / INFRASTRUCTURE CLUES ]
 4248:       x-php-originating-script : 1000:mailer.php
 4249:       Note           : PHP script on shared hosting -- report to hosting abuse team
 4250: 
 4251: One block per detected header, with its note.
 4252: 
 4253: =item 6. Received chain tracking IDs (omitted if no hops have id or for fields)
 4254: 
 4255:     [ RECEIVED CHAIN TRACKING IDs ]
 4256:       (Supply these to the relevant ISP abuse team to trace the session)
 4257: 
 4258:       IP           : 91.198.174.5
 4259:       Envelope for : victim@bandsman.co.uk
 4260:       Server ID    : ABC123XYZ
 4261: 
 4262: Only hops that have at least one of a session ID (C<id>) or envelope
 4263: recipient (C<for>) are shown; IP-only hops are suppressed.  Oldest hop
 4264: first.
 4265: 
 4266: =item 7. Embedded HTTP/HTTPS URLs
 4267: 
 4268:     [ EMBEDDED HTTP/HTTPS URLs ]
 4269:       Host         : bit.ly  *** URL SHORTENER - real destination hidden ***
 4270:       IP           : 67.199.248.11
 4271:       Country      : US
 4272:       Organisation : Bit.ly LLC
 4273:       Abuse addr   : abuse@bit.ly
 4274:       URL          : https://bit.ly/scam123
 4275: 
 4276: URLs are grouped by hostname; if multiple URLs share a hostname, all
 4277: paths are listed together under the single host block.  Known URL
 4278: shorteners are annotated inline.  Shown as C<(none found)> when the
 4279: body contains no HTTP/HTTPS URLs.
 4280: 
 4281: =item 8. Contact / reply-to domains
 4282: 
 4283:     [ CONTACT / REPLY-TO DOMAINS ]
 4284:       Domain       : firmluminary.com
 4285:       Found in     : From: header
 4286:       *** WARNING: RECENTLY REGISTERED - possible phishing domain ***
 4287:       Registered   : 2025-09-01
 4288:       Expires      : 2026-09-01
 4289:       Registrar    : GoDaddy.com LLC
 4290:       Reg. abuse   : abuse@godaddy.com
 4291:       Web host IP  : 104.21.30.10
 4292:       Web host org : Cloudflare Inc
 4293:       Web abuse    : abuse@cloudflare.com
 4294:       MX host      : mail.firmluminary.com
 4295:       MX IP        : 198.51.100.5
 4296:       MX org       : Hosting Corp
 4297:       MX abuse     : abuse@hostingcorp.example
 4298:       NS host      : ns1.cloudflare.com
 4299:       NS IP        : 173.245.58.51
 4300:       NS org       : Cloudflare Inc
 4301:       NS abuse     : abuse@cloudflare.com
 4302: 
 4303: One block per domain from C<mailto_domains()>.  Recently-registered
 4304: domains receive an inline warning banner.  Shown as C<(none found)>
 4305: when no qualifying contact domains are present.
 4306: 
 4307: =item 9. Where to send abuse reports
 4308: 
 4309:     [ WHERE TO SEND ABUSE REPORTS ]
 4310:       Role         : Sending ISP
 4311:       Send to      : abuse@tpg.com.au
 4312:       Note         : Network owner of originating IP 120.88.161.249 (TPG Telecom)
 4313:       Discovered   : ip-whois
 4314: 
 4315:       Role         : Domain registrar for firmluminary.com
 4316:       Send to      : abuse@godaddy.com
 4317:       Note         : Registrar: GoDaddy.com LLC
 4318:       Discovered   : domain-whois
 4319: 
 4320: One block per contact from C<abuse_contacts()>.  Shown as
 4321: C<(no abuse contacts could be determined)> when the list is empty.
 4322: 
 4323: =back
 4324: 
 4325: The report ends with a closing row of 72 equals signs.
 4326: 
 4327: =head3 Side Effects
 4328: 
 4329: Calls C<risk_assessment()>, C<originating_ip()>, C<sending_software()>,
 4330: C<received_trail()>, C<embedded_urls()>, C<mailto_domains()>, and
 4331: C<abuse_contacts()> if they have not already run on the current message,
 4332: performing all associated network I/O as documented in those methods.  All
 4333: underlying results are cached; the report text itself is not cached, but
 4334: re-computation is inexpensive since the data is already available.
 4335: 
 4336: =head3 Notes
 4337: 
 4338: =over 4
 4339: 
 4340: =item *
 4341: 
 4342: The report is idempotent: calling C<report()> multiple times on the same
 4343: object always returns an identical string, because all underlying methods
 4344: are cached.
 4345: 
 4346: =item *
 4347: 
 4348: MIME encoded-words in the C<From:>, C<Subject:>, and other displayed
 4349: headers are decoded for readability.  When a header was encoded, both the
 4350: decoded form and the raw encoded original are shown, so the report is
 4351: useful both for human reading and for log parsing.
 4352: 
 4353: =item *
 4354: 
 4355: URL hosts in section 7 are grouped by hostname and shown in first-seen
 4356: order.  Multiple URLs on the same host are listed together rather than
 4357: repeating the host's IP and WHOIS information, keeping the output compact
 4358: even when a message contains dozens of tracking-pixel and click-redirect
 4359: URLs all on the same CDN.
 4360: 
 4361: =item *
 4362: 
 4363: The received-trail section (section 6) filters out hops that have only an
 4364: IP address and no C<id> or C<for> clause.  The full unfiltered trail is
 4365: available via C<received_trail()>.
 4366: 
 4367: =item *
 4368: 
 4369: Section 5 (sending software) and section 6 (received chain tracking IDs)
 4370: are entirely omitted -- no heading, no placeholder text -- when no relevant
 4371: headers are present.  All other sections always appear, using a
 4372: C<(none found)> or equivalent placeholder when their data is empty.
 4373: 
 4374: =item *
 4375: 
 4376: The version number in the banner is the value of C<$Email::Abuse::Investigator::VERSION>
 4377: at the time C<report()> is called.
 4378: 
 4379: =back
 4380: 
 4381: =head3 API Specification
 4382: 
 4383: =head4 Input
 4384: 
 4385:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 4386:     # No arguments.
 4387:     []
 4388: 
 4389: =head4 Output
 4390: 
 4391:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 4392:     {
 4393:         type  => SCALAR,
 4394:         # Non-empty plain-text string, newline-terminated (\n).
 4395:         # Always defined; never undef.
 4396:         # Line endings: Unix LF (\n) only.
 4397:         # Structure: nine fixed sections in the order documented above,
 4398:         #            separated by blank lines, framed by 72-character
 4399:         #            equals-sign banners.
 4400:     }
 4401: 
 4402: =cut
 4403:

 4619: # _split_message( $text )
 4620: #
 4621: # Entry criteria:
 4622: #   $text  -- a defined scalar containing a raw RFC 2822 email message,
 4623: #             with headers and body separated by a blank line (\n\n or
 4624: #             \r\n\r\n).  Both LF and CRLF line endings are accepted.
 4625: #             The text must already be dereferenced (parse_email() handles
 4626: #             the scalar-ref case before calling this method).
 4627: #             Called only from parse_email(); $self->{_sending_sw} and
 4628: #             $self->{_rcvd_tracking} must have been reset to [] by the
 4629: #             caller before this method is invoked.
 4630: #
 4631: # Exit status:
 4632: #   Returns undef (and does nothing further) if $header_block is undef or
 4633: #   contains only whitespace -- i.e. the message has no headers or is
 4634: #   empty.  Otherwise returns no meaningful value; all results are
 4635: #   communicated through side effects on $self.
 4636: #
 4637: # Side effects:
 4638: #   $self->{_headers}      set to an arrayref of { name => lc, value => raw }
 4639: #                          hashrefs, one per parsed header, in message order.
 4640: #                          Folded (multi-line) values are unfolded before
 4641: #                          parsing (RFC 2822 s2.2.3).  Header names are
 4642: #                          normalised to lower-case.
 4643: #   $self->{_received}     set to an arrayref of raw Received: header values
 4644: #                          (not decoded), in message order (most-recent first).
 4645: #   $self->{_body_plain}   set to the decoded plain-text body.  Appended to
 4646: #                          (not replaced) for each text/* part in multipart
 4647: #                          messages.
 4648: #   $self->{_body_html}    set to the decoded HTML body.  Appended to for
 4649: #                          each text/html part in multipart messages.
 4650: #   $self->{_sending_sw}   populated with { header, value, note } hashrefs
 4651: #                          for any of the six recognised software-fingerprint
 4652: #                          headers that are present, in alphabetical key order.
 4653: #   $self->{_rcvd_tracking} populated with { received, ip, for, id } hashrefs,
 4654: #                          one per Received: hop from which at least one of
 4655: #                          an IP, envelope recipient, or session ID could be
 4656: #                          extracted, in oldest-first order.
 4657: #
 4658: # Notes:
 4659: #   Delegates body decoding to _decode_multipart() for multipart/* content
 4660: #   types (which recursively handles each part) or to _decode_body() for
 4661: #   single-part messages.  If Content-Type is absent or unrecognised, the
 4662: #   body is treated as plain text with 7bit encoding.
 4663: #   The boundary parameter in multipart Content-Type is extracted with a
 4664: #   simple regex; missing or malformed boundaries cause the body to be
 4665: #   silently skipped.
 4666: #   Lines that do not match the header pattern /^([\w-]+)\s*:\s*(.*)/ are
 4667: #   silently discarded (covers blank lines and non-header preamble text).
 4668:

 4805: # _find_origin()
 4806: #
 4807: #   Identifies the IP address of the machine that originally injected the
 4808: #   message into the public mail system by walking the Received: chain and
 4809: #   discarding every hop that belongs to private, reserved, or
 4810: #   caller-trusted infrastructure.  The first remaining (oldest external)
 4811: #   IP is enriched with reverse DNS, organisation, abuse contact, and
 4812: #   country information and returned as the origin hashref.
 4813: #
 4814: #   This is the back-end implementation for the public originating_ip()
 4815: #   method, which caches the result.  _find_origin() itself performs no
 4816: #   caching and must not be called directly; always call originating_ip().
 4817: #
 4818: # Entry criteria:
 4819: #   No arguments beyond $self.
 4820: #   $self->{_received}     must be populated (by _split_message()).
 4821: #   $self->{_headers}      must be populated (by _split_message()), so
 4822: #                          that _header_value('x-originating-ip') works.
 4823: #   $self->{trusted_relays} must be an arrayref (set by new(); may be []).
 4824: #
 4825: # Exit status:
 4826: #   Returns a hashref on success -- the same structure as originating_ip():
 4827: #     { ip, rdns, org, abuse, country, confidence, note }
 4828: #   Returns undef if no usable originating IP can be determined:
 4829: #     -- all Received: IPs are private, reserved, or trusted; AND
 4830: #     -- X-Originating-IP is absent, private, or unparseable.
 4831: #
 4832: # Side effects:
 4833: #   Performs network I/O via _enrich_ip():
 4834: #     - one PTR (rDNS) lookup for the chosen IP (_reverse_dns)
 4835: #     - one RDAP or WHOIS query for the chosen IP (_whois_ip)
 4836: #   No state is written to $self; the result is returned, not cached here.
 4837: #
 4838: # Notes:
 4839: #   The Received: chain is walked in reverse message order -- i.e. from
 4840: #   the oldest header (bottom of the header block) to the most recent --
 4841: #   so that candidates are accumulated in oldest-first order.  $candidates[0]
 4842: #   is always the outermost (first) external relay.
 4843: #
 4844: #   Confidence levels are assigned as follows:
 4845: #     'high'   -- two or more distinct external IPs found in the chain.
 4846: #                 Multiple independent relay records corroborate the origin.
 4847: #     'medium' -- exactly one external IP found in the chain.
 4848: #     'low'    -- no usable Received: IP; origin taken from
 4849: #                 X-Originating-IP, which is injected by webmail providers
 4850: #                 and is not independently verifiable.  Brackets and
 4851: #                 whitespace are stripped from the header value before use.
 4852: #
 4853: #   _is_private() treats undef and the empty string as private, so a
 4854: #   Received: header from which no IP can be extracted is silently skipped
 4855: #   without special-casing.
 4856: #
 4857: #   IPv6 addresses are not extracted by _extract_ip_from_received(); only
 4858: #   dotted-quad IPv4 addresses are considered.
 4859: 
 4860: sub _find_origin {
●4861 → 4864 → 4871●4861 → 4864 → 0 4861: 	my $self = $_[0];
